## Claude

You're right. Bench can't type `/clear` into its own window, and if it restarted its own process it would lose Remote Control, which only you can turn back on. A warning about how full Bench is would only let it write the handoff a little before automatic compaction instead of just after. That isn't worth a hook that runs on every turn, so I withdrew that recommendation.

The real picture: automatic compaction already acts as Bench's restart. A hook you asked for on 10-22 (`~/dev/Lodestar/bin/hooks/compaction_watch.py`) already handles it. Before each compaction it logs whether a handoff was written. Afterwards it tells the resumed Bench to send you a notice and, if the handoff is missing, rebuild one from the transcript on disk.

That hook's log is broken, though. `D:\ClaudeWork\logs\compactions.jsonl` lists 435 compactions under the Bench folder, 203 of them today, and every single one says the handoff is missing. A 100% reading like that is almost certainly the instrument, not the truth. Most likely the helpers working in the same folder are being logged as Bench. I filed it as `COMPACTION_LOG_FIX_HELPERS_COUNTED_AS_SEAT`.

I rewrote the Q1 card. The new recommendation: compaction is the restart, so make it lose nothing:
- Keep Bench thin, with the heavy work done by helpers, so it fills slowly.
- Fix the log so it counts only Bench and correctly sees whether a handoff was written.
- Put "compactions without a handoff" in the daily digest.

Feeding Bench its fill level is now the second option, marked withdrawn. A morning restart that you do by hand is the third. Pushed as `1b70244f0dd4`; the card is in `D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\owner_cards_2026-10-27.md`.

Separately, the fix for the 140 ms Gimme Some Slack rebuild landed offline (`36187e3524`). It had been making about 3.7 MB of garbage per frame, now about 0.29 MB, and the selftests pass. It still needs a live run and a code review of its 8 changed files. Live round 8 starts once the hose fix finishes. That fix is the only helper still running.

## Lukas

No... please look online how perpetual agents handle context. Do they really just permit it to expand until compaction? That's grotequely inefficient. For subagents the master agent allows them to terminate after each action. An orchestrator could have a similar loop. It could orchestrate until the next round of orchestration wouldn't be informed by the previous: time to handoff, die, and restart. Right? That should save a lot of context I'd think. Prove me wrong. Try some simulations in the background (don't use real tokens please for that) to see what could be done. We could for example have a deterministic program periodically ask the agent this answer and if it says "yes, it would be more efficient at this point given what we've done and what's coming for me to handoff...." and then it does. And the deterministic code just looks for a completed handoff, then restarts it. There's got to be things like that out there, right? Please search the web for this information, gather the designs you find and anthropic advice, then ask it again to GPT with all that info and ask it to yet again expand further.

## Claude

I've started two background helpers on this, and I'll bring you their findings when they finish.

1. **Research.** It's searching the web for how always-on agents manage context:
   - Anthropic's own guidance: their context-engineering and long-running-agent posts, auto-compact, the memory tool, clearing old tool results, and subagent and session handling.
   - Loops that restart fresh every round, like the "Ralph" loop.
   - MemGPT/Letta, OpenAI's compaction advice, and any existing scripts that relaunch Claude Code from a handoff file.

   It writes the designs it finds to `D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\perpetual_orchestrator_context_2026-10-26.md`. Then it gives GPT that whole file, asks it to critique and go further, and fact-checks GPT's answer.

2. **Simulation, with no model calls.** It's a plain Python script that compares five ways of running Bench:
   - Let it grow until compaction (what happens today).
   - Hand off and restart when it reaches a set fullness.
   - Your idea: a supervisor program periodically asks "would handing off now be cheaper?", waits for a finished handoff, then restarts Bench. The cost of asking is counted too.
   - Restart after every round, like a subagent.
   - Clear old tool output in place without restarting.

   Turn sizes and idle gaps come from Bench's real transcripts on disk.

The simulation has to include one thing that could prove either of us wrong: prompt caching. Re-reading the earlier conversation costs about a tenth of the normal price while the cache is warm. After 5 minutes idle (or an hour, depending on the setting) the cache expires and the whole conversation is paid for again at full price. A restart also has a fixed cost: about 47,500 tokens measured for a cold start. So whether growing is wasteful depends on these numbers, and the simulation will show where the answer flips. Separately from cost, answers get worse as the conversation gets longer, and the simulation will measure that too.

I need to correct one thing. I told you a restarted Bench would lose Remote Control, and I hadn't checked that. Every window is launched with Remote Control switched on, and the drops on record came from `/login`, not restarts. So a supervisor that relaunches Bench the same way may keep it. The research helper is checking this, and I've rewritten Q1 in the card file to stop claiming it.

## Lukas

Yes, and you can look at our past logs to see how often it would be within that 5 minute warm window when reconsidering a prompt. Epsecially as an orchestrator,  I suspect often not.

## Claude

I've added this to the simulation helper's job. It doesn't need to guess from timing gaps, because every API call in Bench's transcripts records how much of the conversation was read from the warm cache and how much had to be written again in full. It will measure, from Bench's own logs:
- how often a call landed while the cache was still warm and how often it paid to re-send everything, split by how long the gap before it was;
- what share of the money went on those full re-sends.

The simulation will then use those measured rates instead of assumed ones.

One thing might change the answer: this harness may be using the 1-hour cache instead of the 5-minute one. The logs record which cache each write used, so we'll get the real number either way. If Bench is mostly cold, as you suspect, every stale prompt pays the full conversation again, and your restart loop wins. If the 1-hour cache keeps it warm through your pauses, the margin shrinks. I'll bring you the numbers when it finishes.

## Claude

The simulation is finished, and you were right in direction: Bench as it runs today is the expensive case. The research report (web designs plus GPT's critique) is still running.

**What Bench costs now:**
- I measured 49 Bench sessions, about 11,000 API calls.
- A round of orchestration costs about 164k token-units on average.
- In that measure an uncached input token counts as 1, so a cached read counts as about 0.1.
- The average context per call is 251k tokens.

**What the better strategies save:**
- **Hand off at a fixed size, about 125k tokens:** roughly 36% cheaper than today. The context Bench works in is 2.4 to 4.4 times shorter, so its answers should also degrade less.
- **Restart after every round, like a subagent:** about the same cost, with half the context. But it only wins if waking is cheap. If the state it reads on each restart grows to about 16k tokens, it becomes 69% *more* expensive than today.
- **Your probe idea** ("would a handoff be cheaper now?"): within about 4 points of the best fixed threshold. That threshold was picked knowing the answer in advance, and your version needs no tuning. That makes it a sound practical choice.
- **Growing to compaction in a 1M window:** the worst plan, 135% more expensive.

**Your warm-window question:**
- Bench already uses the 1-hour cache. No 5-minute writes appear in the logs.
- Calls made within an hour of the previous one are 99.6–100% warm.
- Calls made after more than an hour are almost all full re-sends, about 7.5% of spend.
- With a 5-minute window your suspicion would hold. A third of the calls that open a new round would miss the cache. They would cost 1.69 times as much, and probing only when the cache has gone cold would become the winning rule (19% cheaper).

**The biggest lever is wake cost, which nobody has examined yet.** Each new session's first call carries about 77k tokens, and only about 27k of that is read from the cache. So every restart writes about 50k tokens fresh. Making a fully cached wake of about 47.5k would push the best threshold down to 75k tokens and save 35%. That makes "what is in those 50k wake tokens, and can it be trimmed" the next thing to find out, before building any supervisor.

Clearing old tool results in place never pays here. Tool output is only 17% of what accumulates, and each clear breaks the cache.

**Files:**
- Results: `D:\ClaudeWork\rimmandrake\perpetual_ctx\sim_results.md` (chart: `D:\ClaudeWork\rimmandrake\perpetual_ctx\sim.png`)
- Simulation script: `D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\src\RimMandrake\Utils\sim\ctx_strategy_sim.py`, pushed. It still needs an independent review and has no self-test.
- The dollar figures assume $5 per million input tokens.

<result>The research is done and pushed. **The honest answer:** no, not everyone lets context grow until compaction. Claude Code's default and chat apps do, but many long-running agent loops keep their state on disk and restart fresh, or prune as they go. Your idea already exists as a mod, claude-auto-handoff, which writes a handoff, runs `/clear` and sends a starting prompt. Anthropic is split. Its docs say to start a new session for each new task, but its 2026-03-24 harness post stopped using resets for Opus 4.5+ and relied on auto-compaction. For BENCH as it actually runs (typical peak about 347k tokens), the sibling simulation puts the cost at about 1.6x the best restart policy, so about 37% is avoidable.

**Best 3 designs:**
1. **D1/D2, in-process handoff mod:** at a safe point (no helpers running, `handoff.py --check` passes, context over a threshold), it writes the handoff, runs `/clear` and submits `wake`. A fixed rule decides, and the agent's opinion is only advice.
2. **D3, outer restart loop:** kill and relaunch BENCH for each unit of work.
3. **D4, early steered `/compact` with instructions:** the fallback.

**What GPT added:**
- **Decision tools:** a cost formula and the rule "write the checkpoint before a likely long idle, not after". In its example that is $0.51 versus $1.68.
- **Safe-switch protocol:** a numbered epoch plus a message cursor, so a phone message arriving between handoff and `/clear` isn't lost. It also suggests rolling checkpoints.
- **How to ask the agent:** don't ask it "would a handoff be more efficient?", since its own verdict isn't reliable. Ask factual questions instead.
- **A correction:** it caught the Anthropic post above, which changed my answer.

Every source and sum it gave that I could check held up, and none was wrong. Unchecked: Magentic-One, two paper revision dates, and its failure-mode list.

**Remote Control after a restart:** a relaunch through the AGENT BENCH profile does keep Remote Control, but it shows up as a new entry on your phone. A mod can run `/clear` itself (`$.command.run`). The docs say `/clear` keeps the same session and only resets the connected devices, so your phone loses the scrollback. That means the owner-cards Q1 claim (BENCH can't `/clear` itself, and a restart loses Remote Control until a human turns it back on) is wrong; I filed that as a lesson. I haven't tested this on this machine; one desk test of `/clear` with Remote Control on 2.1.308 is still owed.

## Claude

You were right, and I was wrong earlier: Bench can hand off, clear itself and restart, and letting it grow until compaction is the expensive way to run it.

**What exists out there:**
- Claude Code's default, and chat apps generally, do let context grow until compaction.
- Many long-running agent loops don't. They keep their state on disk and either restart fresh or trim as they go.
- Your exact idea already exists as a Claude Code mod, claude-auto-handoff. It writes a handoff, runs `/clear` itself, and sends the starting prompt.
- Anthropic is split. Its docs say to start a new session for each new task. But its 2026-03-24 post on long-running harnesses stopped using resets for Opus 4.5 and later and relied on auto-compaction.

For Bench as it actually runs, the simulation says growing costs about 36% more than handing off at around 125k tokens.

**What I had wrong:** a mod can run `/clear` in Bench's own window, and relaunching Bench through its usual launcher keeps Remote Control. Your phone would show the restarted Bench as a new entry. A `/clear` reportedly keeps the same entry, but you'd lose the earlier scrollback. Neither has been tested on this machine yet. I deleted the false claim from the Q1 card (`0542b21aef17`); its options still predate this finding and need redrafting.

**What GPT added, fact-checked:**
- Don't ask the agent "would a handoff be cheaper now?". Its own judgment of that isn't reliable. Let fixed rules decide and ask it only factual questions, such as whether helpers are still running or whether the handoff check passes.
- Write the handoff before a likely long idle, not after the cache has expired. In GPT's example that is $0.51 against $1.68.
- Number each restart, so a phone message that arrives between the handoff and the `/clear` isn't lost.

**The design I'd build:** a mod that restarts Bench at a safe point. It triggers only when all of these are true:
- no helpers are running;
- the handoff check passes;
- the context is past about 125k tokens, or you've gone idle.

It then writes the handoff, runs `/clear` and sends "wake". Before building it, two things:
1. **A desk test of `/clear` with Remote Control on, in this window.** Your phone's view of this conversation would reset to a fresh session. Just say when it's a good moment.
2. **Find out what is in the ~50k tokens each new session writes fresh.** That is the biggest lever on the saving, and I can start it offline now.

- Simulation: `D:\ClaudeWork\rimmandrake\perpetual_ctx\sim_results.md`

<result>Both items are fixed offline and pushed in code commit bf24416b7b; neither has been run live yet, and neither item is closed. The ledger notes and a lesson went out in fda375f2ea. The full FlowWorks selftest passed (1036/1036), including the new HX5 check.

- **What was allocating (item 1):** lead-out candidates each kept a full sampled copy of their path, every lead-out pair re-finished fresh lists, sorting used LINQ OrderBy, RigHits built new sets, the centre join was recomputed for every pair, and the clothoid solver allocated new arrays per node.
- **What changed (item 1):** candidates keep only their compact route description, finished paths come from a per-thread reuse pool, sorts run on reused arrays in the same order, and the solver reuses its thread's buffers. Offline on the 325 basin map a lay now allocates 1.6 MB instead of 4.7 MB, and the A1 check's bound is 2.5 MB. 64 lays produce the same hash before and after, so the lays are byte-identical.
- **What spent the time (item 2):** when a wave of lays lands, every affected hose is re-posed in one frame (pose, Mated up to 50 ms, three Ribbons up to 14 ms each), and every crossing shadow it dirties is rescanned and rebuilt. Each piece is under its bar; the sum is not.
- **What changed (item 2):** DrawAll now spends at most 8 ms per frame re-posing hoses and at most 4 ms on crossing shadows, with at least one of each per frame. Hoses past the budget keep drawing their previous pose and catch up in later frames, so for a few frames a hose can show its old shape. New public counters record how much was deferred (lastDeferredRebuilds / lastDeferredCross, and totals since load). HX5 proves deferred crossing shadows end up the same as a full rescan.
- **What the live re-run must measure:** heap growth in MB/s during lays, full GCs per wave, LayFromOn max, Root_Play.Update max against 100 ms, DrawCrossShadow max against 10 ms, and the deferral counters. It should also check whether any spike that remains coincides with a garbage-collection count increment.
- **Owed:** an independent review of the six changed sources, which all read DIRTY under code_review_status, and both live re-runs before the items can close.
- **Other:** I filed a lesson that string hashes are randomized per run on net8, so they can't be used to compare lays across runs. I skipped the compaction hook's notification and handoff because they are meant for the main seat. The tree is clean apart from other seats' `conversations/*.md` files.

## Lukas

But a 35% savings on orchestrator tokens may be miniscule compared to the subagents, right? Compute whether this question even matters. If we just let compaction run wild for the orchestrator... what's the overall % change in token spend/day given our past behavior? We might be optimizing a tiny piece. And compaction is certainly the easiest... but it might have its own perils too. I'm thinking it seems like the simulations to model might be something like:  1) Smart batching: A) pre-defining related orchestration tasks that are related and can benefit from shared context B) ensuring they don't ever run longer than 1 hour, so that the wake text is still in the 1 hour cache, and C) including some "early handoff" if the task ends up finishing early or other exceptions occur. 2) Guessing a time limit/context window size after which the agent is encouraged to "finish work but handoff soon." or 3) Just let it compact and handle itself (may or may not be within the 1 hour window, use our actual past behavior to estimate). Again, this should be viewed entirely from the "how many tokens would this save as a percentage of how much we already burn?" to ensure we're not wasting our time polishing a cannonball.

## Claude

I've given this to the simulation helper. The first thing it will measure is the total: every session on this machine over the last ~7 days, split into Bench itself, Bench's helpers, Foundry and its helpers, and the other projects (Hestia, Lodestar, the test-queue runs and so on). That shows what share of daily spend Bench's own conversation actually is.

It will then express every option as a percentage of that total, not of Bench's own spend:
1. **Smart batching:**
   - groups of related tasks run together;
   - each group is capped at an hour, so the starting text is still in the 1-hour cache;
   - an early handoff if the group finishes early or something goes wrong.
2. **A soft time or size limit** after which Bench is told to finish up and hand off.
3. **Let it compact on its own**, using our real past behaviour, including how often a resume after compaction landed inside or outside the 1-hour cache.

It will also measure the risks of compaction that our logs can show: a handoff missing after a compaction, next-step pointers that got lost, and files re-read afterwards. Anything it can't measure will be marked as such.

If the helpers turn out to dominate spend, which I expect, it will say so plainly and rank the three biggest levers on helper spend instead.

Also running:
- **Live round 8:** the hose memory-cleanup and relay-batch fixes, the 140 ms Gimme Some Slack rebuild, the press over-limit stacks, and the three cold-storage checks still owed.
- **An independent code review** of all of today's changed files.
- **What fills the ~50k-token cost of starting a fresh session.** That work only matters if this question turns out to matter, and the answer above will tell us.

## Claude

The breakdown of what a fresh Bench session costs to start is done. It matters less than it looked, so I'm holding these cuts until the "does it matter overall" answer comes back.

**Where the ~71k tokens of a session's first call go:**
- **About 29k is read from cache:** the system prompt and tool definitions. The Artifact tool alone is about 12k of that.
- **About 42k is written fresh every time:**
  - the skills list (127 entries): about 11.7k;
  - the repo CLAUDE.md: about 9.8k;
  - the global CLAUDE.md: about 7.2k;
  - the memory index: about 5.5k;
  - the environment section, the agent list and Bench's identity file: about 5.4k together.
- **The wake output adds about 7k** on the next call.

These are estimates: no tokenizer is installed, so sizes are characters ÷ 4, scaled to real token counts. The script is still unreviewed.

**Why none of that is cache-hit between sessions:** the git status block sits right after the instruction files and changes every session. The scratchpad path also contains the session ID. Everything after the first byte that differs is written fresh. That conclusion comes from the request layout and the usage numbers, not a direct test. I found no setting that stops the git status from being inserted, so trimming text is the only lever.

**The three biggest cuts:**
1. Cap each skill description at about 200 characters: saves about 7k.
2. Trim the repo CLAUDE.md to pointers plus your hard rulings: saves about 5.5k.
3. Move the sections of the global CLAUDE.md that Bench doesn't need (Lodestar, Dialexis, work, Bearing, publications) somewhere else: saves about 3.5k.

Together that is about 16k off each restart, or about 38% of the fresh write. Whether it's worth doing depends on what share of total spend Bench's conversation is. Details are in `D:\ClaudeWork\rimmandrake\perpetual_ctx\wake_breakdown.md`.

Still running: the "does it matter overall" measurement, live round 8, and the code review.

## Claude

You were right: Bench's own conversation is a cannonball we'd be polishing. The best orchestrator strategy saves only about **2.9% of total daily spend**.

**Where the spend goes (last 7 days, about 270M token-units a day):**

| Who | Share of total |
|---|---|
| Subagents | 83.7% |
| Bench's own conversation | 6.6% |
| Foundry's own conversation | 2.4% |
| Headless runs | about 7% |

These are token-units: an uncached input token counts 1 and a cached read about 0.1.

**Your three options, as a share of total spend:**

| Option | Change in total spend |
|---|---|
| Smart batching (≤55 min sessions, early handoff) | −2.5% |
| Soft limit at 100k–150k ("finish and hand off soon") | −2.6% to −2.7% |
| Plain compaction in a 200k window | −2.3% |
| Compaction left to run wild in the 1M window (fires at about 750k) | **+7.1%** |

The one rule worth keeping is that Bench never runs past about 150k before compacting or handing off. Which of the three methods does that barely matters, so the easiest one, compaction in a 200k window, is fine. Whether grouping related tasks helps (part A of smart batching) can't be measured from the transcripts.

**Compaction's risks, measured:**
- 7 compactions, all automatic. Each fired about 55 minutes into an idle gap, just before the cache expired.
- Each resume cost about one cold restart. All 7 together came to 0.04% of total spend.
- Lost next-step pointers and drift after compaction can't be measured.

**Where the money actually is, in subagents:**
1. **Long-running helpers:** re-reading their own context past 100k tokens is **19.1% of all spend**. Splitting or compacting them around 100k would recover much of that; our helper types compact at 150k today. Filed as `HELPER_CONTEXT_SPLIT_AT_100K_REREADS_COST_A_FIFTH`.
2. **Opus helpers are 74% of all spend.** Each third moved to Sonnet would save about 10%, if the quality holds, which nobody has measured. Which model does which work is your ladder in `Agent_Policy.md`, so that one is your call. I haven't touched it.
3. **Helpers use the 5-minute cache.** Any wait inside a helper longer than 5 minutes rewrites its whole context, and the current 9-minute timeout allows that. This costs **3.8%**. Capping waits under 5 minutes fixes it; switching helpers to the 1-hour cache would cost more than it saves. Filed as `HELPER_WAITS_FIX_5MIN_CACHE_REWRITES`.

My recommendation: drop the orchestrator restart work, and don't act on the cuts to Bench's startup text either, since they only matter inside that 2.9%. Spend the effort on item 1 next. Both items are filed and pushed (`57eed0cc89fd`); say if you want item 1 started now.

- When you've been idle about 45 minutes, it sends Bench "prepare handoff". The cache is still warm, so writing the handoff is cheap.
- Once the handoff is committed, it runs `/clear` and then "wake".
- During active work, a soft limit around 150k means "hand off at the next stopping point" instead of letting compaction cut in.

That replaces lossy idle compactions with real handoffs at roughly zero token cost. The open risk is the untested `/clear` with Remote Control on, which needs one test while you're here. Your phone's view would reset. Shall I build the supervisor and do that test now?

## Claude

Yes, your split matches the research, and it's very close to the single-primary design already waiting on cards D1–D7. One change makes it better: the orchestrator shouldn't be a long-lived AI conversation at all, so nothing ever has to compact freely.

**The research supports it:**
- **Keep the human-facing agent apart from the autonomous work.** Anthropic's advice is to clear the context between unrelated tasks, keep heavy work in subagents, and keep state in files rather than in a conversation. A desk agent whose context you control is exactly that.
- **Compaction works for long-running autonomous agents.** Anthropic's own March 2026 post on long-running agents stopped restarting Opus and let it compact. So an autonomous agent compacting is acceptable.
- **The cost is small.** Bench's whole conversation is 6.6% of daily spend. Your messages are re-read at about a tenth of the price while the cache is warm.

**The refinement:** the strongest pattern in the research is "state on disk, fresh workers", like the Ralph loop and Anthropic's harnesses. The single-primary design already does it that way:
- The **engine** is a systemd timer, the same kind as the test queue that already runs every 60 s.
- It starts **one fresh worker per item**. Each worker dies when its item is done, so it never compacts.
- Everything passes through the ledger, never by message.

So nothing needs a compacting brain. If the engine ever needs judgment, such as triage, it's a short fresh call that reads the files. That's the cheapest option, and it removes the "lost next step after compaction" risk entirely.

**What that makes the agent you talk to:** Bench becomes the desk.
- It does only what you ask, spawning helpers for your requests.
- **You decide when its context resets.** Say "hand off" and then `/clear`, or let it compact if you prefer.
- **Foundry's window retires**, as D1 proposes. The engine takes over its queue.

The only thing in your version I'd drop is a freely compacting "Orchestrator" agent. The timer and fresh workers do that job better and never compact.

**The open question is how well compaction keeps what matters.** I've started that test now:
- **Part A uses no new tokens.** It audits our real compactions against the seven things CLAUDE.md says a compaction summary must keep. It scores each as kept word for word, paraphrased, or lost. It also checks whether the global CLAUDE.md comes back afterwards; Claude Code's docs promise only the project file and the memory index.
- **Part B is a small controlled probe in a throwaway session.** It plants rulings, next steps, running helpers and a mid-session correction, forces a compaction, and quizzes recall. A script grades the answers, not a model.

Nothing touches `/clear` or Remote Control. The results will be in `D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\compaction_retention_test_2026-10-26.md`.

If the results are good, your choice to just let this window grow is sound for now. If they're bad, it's the evidence for putting the D1–D7 cards to you sooner. Live round 8 is still running.

## Lukas

Ok, so who writes the plans that the non-AI engine follows?

## Claude

- `Bash — Find planning/priority rules in the design`

## Claude

Nobody writes a plan today, and that's the gap your Q3–Q5 questions point at. The engine doesn't follow a plan. It follows the queue.

**How it works in the design:**
- **The tick, with no AI, every 2 minutes.** It counts running workers against its slot allowance. For each free slot it asks the ledger for the next ready item. The ledger ranks by fixed rules: the work stream, then items that unblock others, then age. It has no judgement and no notion of a goal.
- **Who writes the items in that queue:**
  1. Bench, with you: your requests, design work, rulings.
  2. Workers: each files what it finds ("this broke", "this is owed").
  3. The hourly triage pass: one short, fresh Sonnet run that reads the hour's results. It unblocks stuck items with a better brief or a stronger model, drops stale ones, and drafts cards for you.

So the engine grinds down whatever the queue holds, in a generic order. That's how we end up making slow progress across many mods, which is your complaint.

**What the cards add, so there is a plan:**
- **Focus lane (Q3):** 1–2 named mods, each with a written finish list. Items in the lane outrank everything else in the ledger's ordering, so the tick follows your focus without understanding it.
- **"After" links (Q4):** when work is filed, it records what it waits on. The order then becomes something a program can compute: what's ready, what's blocked, the critical path.
- **Monday week-plan card (Q5):** at most 10 items. An item is ticked only when its check passes, recorded by someone other than its builder.

**Who writes the plan, then:** I recommend Bench drafts it, from the finish lists, the "after" links and last week's results, and you approve or change it on the Monday card. After that the engine only executes it: the focus lane outranks everything else, and blocked items wait.

The thinking happens at the desk with you, once a week. The timer does the grinding without needing to understand anything, and triage handles exceptions hourly.

This should go on the Q3–Q5 cards when you're ready to take them.

## Claude

Compaction keeps more than I expected. It almost always keeps your words, but it loses the dates on your rulings, exact item IDs and helper IDs. The test covered 18 real compactions of Bench and Foundry, plus a small controlled test that cost about $1.43.

| What must survive | Real compactions | Controlled test, with our compaction instructions | Without them |
|---|---|---|---|
| Your words, quoted exactly | 93% (316/339) | 5/5 | 5/5 |
| The **date** on each ruling | **3%** | 5/5 | 0/5 (recalled as "today") |
| Open item IDs, exact | **52%** (some shortened so a search can't find them) | 5/5 | 5/5 |
| Running helper IDs | 4/20 | 3/3 | 0/3 |
| "Handoff owed" flag | 5 of 6 since our instructions were added | 1/1 | 0/1 |
| Who holds the game controls | 9/9 | kept | kept |

- **Your words survive because of Claude Code itself.** Its built-in summary lists every user message. Our compaction instructions in CLAUDE.md are what save the dates, the handoff flag and the helper IDs.
- **After a compaction, the startup instructions all come back.** That includes both CLAUDE.md files (global in 12 of 12 cases), the memory index, loaded skills and the list of running helpers. Your worry about losing them doesn't hold.
- **The worst real loss:** in one Bench session your rail-ladder ruling ("North needs to hug the wall perspective tighter…") vanished completely. So did the next step of an open item, `OFFLINE_PILE_DEPLOY_ONE_TRIP_FOR_ALL`.
- **No harm showed afterwards** on rough checks: no question re-asked of you and no work redone.
- **The controlled test was the easy case.** Its history was about 65k tokens; real Bench sessions run 215k–588k before compacting.

So letting this window grow is acceptable. The fix is the same for all three weak spots: keep that state on disk and add it back after compaction, rather than trusting the summary. Concretely:
- re-insert the open items, read from the ledger;
- keep your rulings with their dates in a file on disk;
- re-insert the IDs of running helpers.

These are filed as `COMPACTION_RESTORE_OPEN_ITEMS_FROM_LEDGER`, `OWNER_RULINGS_HARDEN_DATES_ON_DISK` and `HELPER_IDS_RESTORE_AFTER_COMPACTION` (pushed, `677c3cf7fcb4`). Each is small and offline, and they make compaction close to safe for the window you talk to. Say if you want them built now.

## Claude

You're seeing the gap correctly: the design has no one who re-plans.

**What the design says happens when a worker hits a surprise:**
1. The worker files what it found as a new item ("this is broken") and stops. Its own item is marked failed or blocked.
2. After two failures, the engine blocks that item.
3. Once an hour, a short fresh AI session (the "triage pass") reads the failures. It can rewrite a brief, retry with a stronger model, drop something stale, or draft a card for you.
4. Later tickets that waited on the failed one simply wait.

That's mending, not planning. Nobody asks the questions that matter:
- Does this bug block the goal?
- Which fix ticket goes in, and what waits on it?
- Are the later tickets still right, now that we've learned this?
- Is the goal itself still right?

So the plan grinds to a halt, as you said. Meanwhile the desk sits there waiting for you.

**What today actually looks like.** That re-planning is exactly what Bench did this afternoon. Round 7 found four bugs. I spawned three fixers, a code review caught one more, and I set up round 8 to test them all. The job is real and it's the valuable part. Taking it away from Bench without replacing it is the hole in the cards.

**The missing piece is a planner that restarts each time.** It's your own earlier idea, applied where it actually fits:
- **The plan is a file, not a conversation.** You and the desk write it together: the goal ("RimFlow does X, validated offline, tested live, empirical proof"), the proof that counts as done, the tickets, and what waits on what.
- **The engine wakes the planner on events,** not on a clock: a worker finished, a worker found a surprise, or nothing has moved in a while.
- **The planner is a fresh session each time.** It reads the plan file, the results and the findings. It re-plans: insert the fix ticket, re-link the dependents, drop tickets the surprise made obsolete. It writes down why, and then it exits.
- **It never compacts, because it never lives long.** Everything it knows is on disk, so it's cheap. A planning round is a fraction of the ~50k tokens it costs to start any session, and helpers are 84% of spend anyway.
- **It comes to you only when the goal itself is in question,** for example "this surprise means the design is wrong." Everything else it decides and logs, as with any delegated call.
- **The desk can also play planner while you're present.** It reads the same file, so nothing is lost between the two.

So the roles become:
- **Desk:** you and Bench decide what and why.
- **Planner:** fresh each time, keeps the plan true as reality pushes back.
- **Engine:** a dumb timer, launches whatever is ready.
- **Workers:** one ticket each.

The plan grinds to a halt only when the planner decides it needs you.

The cards were wrong because they treated planning as a weekly list. Your actual cycle is "goal → tickets → surprises → re-plan → proof", run continuously. If this matches your view, I'll rewrite the design around the plan file and the planner first, and only then bring back the questions that are still open.

## Claude

You're right to push back. A deterministic engine is not better at the Manager's job. It's better at a few dumb jobs that a long-lived Opus window does badly. Those are separate questions, and the cards mixed them up.

**What a long-lived Opus Manager does well, and what we'd lose with fresh sessions:**
- **It remembers why.** This afternoon I tied together round 7's four bugs, three fixers, one review finding and round 8 without re-reading anything. A fresh Manager has to rebuild that from files every time it wakes, and some unwritten understanding never makes it to disk.
- **It's cheap.** The orchestrator's own conversation is under 10% of spend. Restarting it saves about 3% of the total, at best.
- **Compaction is mostly survivable.** It keeps your words 93% of the time. The losses (dates, exact item IDs, helper IDs) can be fixed by re-inserting state from disk afterwards.

**What a long-lived window does badly. Every measured failure is here:**
1. **It sleeps when nothing pokes it.** Today Bench is woken only by your messages and by its own helpers finishing. With no helpers running and no you, it waits forever; Foundry once went 23 hours silent. Claude Code has a self-wake loop (`/loop`) that could fix this inside the window.
2. **When the window itself dies or hangs, nobody notices.** Its helpers die with it, and its self-wake loop dies too. Only something outside the window can detect that and restart it.
3. **Dead and stuck workers go unnoticed.** That's your "sometimes they die" problem. Of the 123 items marked "doing", none is actually held by anyone. A Manager inside a window can only see helpers it launched itself, in this session. A timer can check claims and process IDs for everything, every 2 minutes, without fail.
4. **Hard limits need a guard outside the agent.** Helper caps, budgets and "one worker per C# project" all slip when an agent is merely asked to obey them. A hook or program can refuse instead, like the helper-cap hook that refused me today.

**So my recommendation:**
- **Manager:** a long-lived Opus window, as Bench orchestrates today. It wakes itself on a loop to assess the sprint, files tickets and keeps the plan true. It never changes the sprint goal.
- **A small watchdog program:**
  - wakes the Manager if it has gone quiet;
  - restarts it from its handoff if it dies or hangs;
  - catches dead or stuck workers and puts their tickets back in the queue;
  - enforces the caps;
  - re-inserts open items, dated rulings and running helpers after each compaction.
- **Bench:** your desk, to the side. You check in, assess, and design goals and capabilities with it.

That is nearly today's two-window system, which is now tagged as the baseline. Foundry becomes the Manager with a wake loop, the watchdog is added around both windows, and nothing is torn out. It's much less to build than the engine design, and it keeps the part you found works: Opus orchestrating.

Whether the Manager should restart fresh each round or live long is now a smaller question we can measure later. We'd compare stalls per sprint and rework rate on two sprints.

## Lukas

So it feels like our design is collapsing under scrutiny. Seems like what's remaining is - Foundry is a good idea, it builds and has sub-agents that just work tickets. - When Foundry's context crosses a threshold, it replaces the next ticket item with "wait until all work finishes, then write handoff." - If Foundry doesn't write a timeout within 30 mins of being told to do so gently, it is told to do so immediately. - Once a handoff is written, Foundry is killed and restarted fresh from the handoff. Foundry is the orchestrator that ensures the subagents do their job and does local replanning. It would be authorized to add to or modify tickets given subagent behavior.  But now it is monitored so it won't silently stop, blow its context window, or be victim to a stuck subagent. - The "engine" is just a loop to ensure Foundry is active, has subagents working, and has sufficient context left. - Bench goes right back to its original design: working on design items with me, debugging really tricky problems Foundry + subagents failed at, and live human testing. That's what I'm seeing. Please push back or correct me. Once we settle on this, I'm going to want you to go back and simulate how our past performance would have improved given this setup. Oh, and the "Engine" would also have the job to ensure no sub-agents blow their own context or run forever, as has happened, if that's even possible.

## Claude

I agree with the shape. It's the tagged baseline plus a watchdog, which is the least risky change. I'd correct six points before building it.

1. **"Wait until all work finishes" can take an hour.** Rounds 7 and 8 each ran 55–63 minutes. When the threshold trips, Foundry should stop launching new helpers and only receive reports until they're done. That means setting the trip point low enough to leave room: hand off at about 110k, before auto-compaction can fire, which the simulation put near 125–150k. The draining has to happen because Foundry's helpers live inside its process: killing Foundry kills them.

2. **How the watchdog "tells" Foundry is the real engineering risk.** Typing into its window from outside isn't reliable. What can work:
   - **A hook inside Foundry reads a flag file.** On every tool call or turn end, it injects "drain and hand off", or "hand off now" after 30 minutes.
   - **A self-wake loop**, Claude Code's `/loop`, so an idle Foundry still takes a turn and sees the flag.

   This must be tested before anything depends on it.

3. **Runaway subagents can be watched but not killed directly.**
   - **Watching works:** every helper writes its own transcript file, so the watchdog can read each helper's context size, time since its last activity, and total run time.
   - **Killing doesn't, from outside:** a helper runs inside Foundry's process, and only Foundry can stop it (it has a tool for that). So the watchdog flags it and the hook tells Foundry "stop helper X", with a hard ceiling if Foundry won't.
   - **Two guards already exist:** a silence kill after 600 s, and helper definitions that compact at 150k. The new part is a maximum run time and the stuck-helper check.

4. **Foundry needs the sprint goal in a file.** It can add or change tickets but not redefine the goal. That rule only works if the goal and its proof of done are written down where Foundry and the watchdog can both read them. That file is what you and Bench produce at the desk.

5. **The live game is one shared resource.** Bench's live testing with you and Foundry's live rounds both need the game. Rule: when you're present, Bench gets the game, and Foundry's live work waits or stays offline.

   This also means reversing your 10-23 ruling that Bench owns all of FlowWorks. You made it "to prevent mutual waiting". With Foundry watched and never silently stalled, that reason mostly goes away, but it's your ruling to change.

6. **This fixes reliability, not cost.** Subagents are 84–88% of spend, and the watchdog doesn't touch that. Expect fewer stalls, dead helpers and lost handoffs, not a smaller bill.

With those six, I'd call it settled:
- **Bench** is your desk for design, the hard bugs and live testing with you.
- **Foundry** orchestrates and re-plans within the sprint goal, and restarts from a handoff at a context limit.
- **The watchdog** keeps Foundry alive, busy and within limits, and does the same for its helpers.

For the replay of past performance once you confirm, I'd measure from the last month's ledger and transcripts:
- Foundry's idle gaps, and how many hours the watchdog would have recovered.
- Helpers that ran away or got stuck, and what a ceiling would have saved.
- Compactions that lost a handoff, and items stalled waiting on a dead dependency.

Each result would be in hours and items closed per day, not just tokens.

## Lukas

Ok... so tell me... why is this better than just having only Foundry and I talk to Foundry too? Just one window, doing its thing, monitored... and I talk to it sometimes?

## Claude

- `Bash — Read why the two-window design was adopted`

## Claude

I can't defend two windows as clearly better. One monitored window, which you talk to sometimes, is a real option. This session has been exactly that: I ran rounds 7 and 8 and their fixes while designing this with you, and it worked.

**What one window gains:**
- **No coordination.** There are no seats, no lanes, no fight over the live game, and no "who owns FlowWorks". Your 10-23 ruling existed only because two windows waited on each other.
- **One place holds everything:** the sprint, the findings, and your reasons. Nothing is lost passing work between windows.
- **Half the rules and tools to maintain.**

**What a second window buys:**
1. **Your conversation never gets force-restarted.** The watchdog's restart rule fits a queue runner. In one window it would fire mid-conversation, and because every helper report also fills that context, it fills faster and restarts more often while you're talking.
2. **Nothing waits behind your conversation.** A window takes one turn at a time. While it thinks through a design with you, helper reports queue up unhandled, and while it handles them, you wait. Today you got round-8 reports in the middle of the design discussion. That's the cost, though it was small today.
3. **A long design or live-testing session doesn't eat the orchestrator's context.**

None of the three is a failure we've measured as costly. They're risks.

**My recommendation: one window, with two rules that remove the first two risks:**
- **No forced restart while you're active.** If you've spoken in the last 30 minutes, the watchdog only asks for a handoff at the next break. You can also say "hand off" yourself at any time.
- **When you speak, you come first.** Helper reports are handled between your turns, briefly, and summarized rather than relayed one by one.

Then let the data decide whether a second window is worth bringing back. That means counting how many restarts landed during a conversation and how long reports sat waiting behind you. The baseline tag keeps the two-window system one checkout away.

For the replay of past performance, I'd compare three setups:
- today's two windows, as they actually ran;
- two windows with a watchdog;
- one window with a watchdog.

Each would be scored on idle hours recovered, stuck helpers caught, handoffs lost, and items closed per day.

<result>The fix is committed and published at **35b8ab9858**, and all GSS selftests pass (870/870). The slices over 20 ms were not GSS work.
- **Cause:** the worst non-GC slice (86 ms, site B lamps) also froze the lay worker (LayEdge 91 ms at the same moment). No gen-0 collection was counted and nothing was laid on the caller, so it was a pause of the whole process. The likeliest cause is Unity's incremental GC, which `CollectionCount(0)` does not see. The 13–20 ms snapshot Base peaks fit the same pattern, since a full scan takes about 5 ms. The phases next to each window max were the last rebuild's, so the max slice's own phase split was never recorded.
- **Fix:** fewer slices instead of a faster one. `CordBuilder.LayReadyCoalesced` now holds a rebuild until 64 pieces have landed, the oldest is 100 ms old, a graph lands or the batch ends. Before this, the game rebuilt (snapshot copy, Build and tail) on every frame where a piece landed, 40–50 times a second. Offline the same cold build took 34 rebuilds instead of 167 and ended bit-identical.
- **Tests:** two new checks in `SliceGateChecks.cs`, `Coalesce` (the gate's states on a test-driven clock, rebuild count and identical end state) and `Window` (the max record keeps its own phases). The new pure class is `Core\SliceWindow.cs`. The full suite passed 870/870 on the second run. The first run had one failure, the known flaky wall-clock bound in the slice check at 21.0 ms against 20; I left it as is.
- **Round 9 must measure:** in `motion`, the new `sliceWindow` (the max, max-without-GC and first slice). For each it gives the phases (with snapshot Clone timed apart from Base), the untimed gap, gen 0/1/2 and heap changes, whether either worker was busy, and the slice's ordinal and time since slicereset. Also read `coalesceHolds`, `gcIncremental` and `winSlices` per spawn. ## Claude

A quick update, so it doesn't interrupt our design discussion. The Gimme Some Slack fix for round 9 is in (`35b8ab9858`), and all 870 selftests pass.

The 86 ms building steps weren't Gimme Some Slack's own work. During those moments the whole game process paused, most likely for a memory clean-up the game's usual counter doesn't see. So the fix makes fewer building steps rather than faster ones: the game now waits until a batch of pieces has landed before rebuilding, instead of rebuilding up to 50 times a second. Offline, the same build took 34 rebuilds instead of 167 and came out identical. Round 9 will check whether any step still goes over 20 ms.

The 8 changed files still need a code review. The hose fix is still running.

## Claude

The design write-up is done and pushed, together with GPT's case against change. GPT's verdict: the evidence supports better measurement and small repairs, not a new architecture.

`D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\orchestration_design_record_2026-10-26.md`

**GPT's three strongest arguments (checked against our data):**
1. **Nothing measures what you actually care about.** We have no record of a mod being finished, of your time spent checking, or of how good the workers' or planner's work is. The two-window system works, so anything replacing it has to prove itself first.
2. **Idle hours are lost only if tokens were left over and work was ready to run.** If the weekly tokens run out regardless, waking agents sooner just brings the lockout forward.
3. **The best-evidenced problem is information lost at compaction.** That calls for the three small fixes already filed, which put state back from disk after a compaction, not a new system. It also caught a flaw in our simulation: the +7.1% penalty for "compaction run wild" assumed compactions near 750k tokens, but the 7 real ones fired at 222k–491k.

**Your premise, measured: "we always use up all our tokens" is only partly true.**
- Over 33 days there were 3 weekly-limit lockouts and 1 five-hour lockout, plus a few stretches where we throttled ourselves.
- About 103 hours had both windows idle outside every limit period.
- Twice (10-11 and today) you asked for more spending before a weekly reset.
- Whether tokens were actually left over at each reset can't be measured yet, because the usage meter only started at 12:47 today. I've filed that measurement (`QUOTA_LEFT_AT_RESET_MEASURE_PER_ACCOUNT`).

**Where GPT was wrong:** it disputed the dates-on-rulings figure. The figure holds: all 316 of your quotes carried a timestamp that could have been kept. It was right about one thing, though: those 316 are quotes from you, not all of them rulings, and the write-up now says so.

**What GPT missed:** the token answer changes week to week, and none of these designs touches the 84–88% of spend that goes to subagents.

**The design in brief:**
1. **No silent stopping.** A window may only end a turn in one of four declared states:
   - running;
   - ready for the next item;
   - waiting, saying on whom and until when;
   - paused, with the reason.

   A small timer then triggers the "what did we learn, what's next, what's the plan" cycle. GPT itself says the channel for waking an idle window is unproven and must be tested first.
2. **One plan file per goal,** pointing at ledger items. An item can't be closed while checks it owes are still outstanding.
3. **Testing as a cost to cut:** reuse a test's result while the code it covers hasn't changed, and refuse an identical rerun. Keep a list of known nightly failures instead of pretending the nightly run is green.
4. **Art gets a budget per subject** and keeps the best drawing so far. After repeated facing failures it switches method instead of drawing again.
5. **The dead inbox becomes a prepared sit-down.** Each weekday you get 20 minutes covering at most 5 decisions, each read by an agent first.

**GPT's three first steps, each with a target:**

## Claude

You're right on both counts, and they come from the same gap: the system has no written statement of what "working" means from a player's point of view.

**Why those bugs got past the tests.** Every bug on your list is something a player notices in a second. Our tests mostly checked other things: frame times, counts, hashes, whether a script reached its end, and spot checks of hand-picked cases. Nothing ever asserted things like:
- a ladder's art leans toward the wall it touches;
- someone inside a pit can reach every part of the pit floor;
- two connected pipe cells draw as one joined piece;
- a hose leaves its port in the port's direction;
- connected water levels out, and none is created or lost.

Each of these is a plain sentence you'd agree with in a second. I'll call them player-truths.

**Can we test for them?** Mostly yes. Four of your five are pure geometry and logic:
- whether a pit's floor can be reached from inside it;
- whether pipes are joined;
- which way a hose end points;
- whether the water levels out.

Those can be swept exhaustively offline: every facing, every pit shape up to some size, every ladder spot. That's your 10-24 rule ("solve the geometry offline in Python, sweep the whole space") applied as a test. Ladder perspective is a rule about facing that can be checked directly, and a judge agent can confirm it on rendered images.

So the testing scheme becomes:
1. **Each feature carries a list of player-truths.** Every embarrassing bug you find is a missing truth, and it gets added to the list for good.
2. **The truths are swept exhaustively offline,** not spot-checked.
3. **Truths that only show on screen** are rendered and judged by an agent that didn't build the feature.
4. **You only see it when every truth passes.**

**Why this is also what goal-oriented means.** You're right that GPT gave you a schedule of check-ins, not a goal. What makes fire-and-forget possible is a goal the system can measure itself against:
1. **You state the goal:** "pits work the way a player expects."
2. **The system drafts the player-truths.** You review that list once, which is cheap and worth your time, because you're judging the specification rather than every output.
3. **The loop runs on its own:** build, sweep, fix, until the truths pass.
4. **"What did we learn, what's next, what's the plan" has a scoreboard:** truths passing, X of Y. That's also your real tick-off from earlier: a truth ticks only when its check passes.
5. **Your sit-down becomes a final walkthrough**, not keeping the machine going.

**To answer "can we even?" with evidence rather than argument,** I've started an offline pilot on pits and ladders, which will also cover pipe joins, hose ends and pit water if time allows. It will:
- write their player-truths, starting with your five complaints;
- find which are already checked;
- build exhaustive offline sweeps for the rest;
- run them against today's code and file every violation it finds.

That tells us how many of your five complaints this scheme would have caught before you ever saw them, and what bugs exist right now. The write-up will be at `D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\player_truth_testing_pilot_2026-10-26.md`.

## Claude

I think it's the right tool, and it fits the player-truths work. Feeling stuck is partly because nothing today answers "how done is FlowWorks?" Without that answer every day feels the same, however much actually moved.

**Two lessons from earlier attempts:**
1. **Compute it only from evidence.** The old maturity dashboard failed because agents set its levels with a command; its last data said 0 of 58 features had been played. Every number must come from something that happened: a check passed, art accepted, a walkthrough logged.
2. **Measure two different things, not one.**
   - **Coverage:** how much of the mod's content is verified working.
   - **Settledness:** whether reviews and walkthroughs still keep finding big problems.

   A mod can be 90% covered and still unsettled. That's FlowWorks today: round 8 still found three new bugs.

**What the assessment would read, per mod:**
- **Content inventory:** every building, item, creature and mechanic the mod defines, taken from its defs and design.
- **Works:** player-truths passing, of those written, plus live checks at L2 or higher.
- **Art:** accepted, plausible and actually used, against still waiting, a placeholder, or stuck redrawing.
- **Waiting on you:** reviews and picks pending, and for how long.
- **Settledness trends:**
  - defects found per code review;
  - big problems per live walkthrough;
  - bugs filed per week.

  These should fall as the mod nears done.
- **The headline:** "% verified working", "% of art accepted", and a settledness signal ("still finding bugs", "slowing", "quiet"), with a confidence level. Not one magic number, because one number would hide the unknown unknowns.

The design won't need much new data. The ledger, review status, art pipeline, sheet decisions and live-round evidence already hold most of it. What's missing is content inventories per mod and player-truths, and the pilot running now is building the first of those.

All four of my helper slots are busy, so I've filed the work (`MOD_COMPLETION_MEASURE_HOW_DONE_IS_DONE`, with your words) and will start it the moment one frees up. It will be piloted on FlowWorks first, so you can judge whether the numbers match your gut before it becomes a skill for every mod.

## Claude

The independent review of today's Python tools found real bugs in seven files. All are fixed and pushed except one pricing error in the simulation, which is filed.

- **The new selftest cache could have reused a stale pass.** The FlowWorks stock test also reads 10 neighbouring mod folders the cache wasn't watching, so a change there wouldn't have forced a re-run. Fixed, and the cache's own test now fails if a selftest reads a mod folder the cache isn't watching.
- **The pipe elbow fix script wasn't safe to run twice.** A second run would have shifted the now-aligned elbows the other way. It now skips sets that already pass and refuses any result that still fails. The installed art wasn't affected.
- **The completion measure gave some evidence to the wrong mod,** and counted some evidence more than once. Fixed.
- **The context simulation charged a restart twice.** Fixed. It also prices uncached input at twice the real rate. That needs recalibrating against the transcripts, so it's filed rather than fixed, and the "under 3% of total spend" conclusion should be read with that in mind.
- **Smaller fixes:** a miscount in the truth sweep, a crash on blank messages in the friction count, and a wrong Opus flag in the spend count.

That makes five of today's new tools that their own authors called finished and an independent reviewer found broken. That's the case for the "reviewer is never the author" rule I filed earlier.

Still running: the review of today's game code, which comes before live round 10, and the search for the next decisions to delegate.

## Claude

All three delegations are approved, and a helper is now writing them into the rules:
1. **Sheet marks:** your newest mark wins. An ✕ moves the picture to the art archive, where one command restores it, instead of deleting it.
2. **Bridge:** a window may take the free bridge, deploy and restart the game when no other window holds it and you haven't touched the game for 20 minutes. Never for flyer flight.
3. **Cleanup:** agents may delete our own leftovers once a check proves nothing reads them and everything is pushed. Each is archived or bundled first.

Every one of these acts gets listed in your daily digest.

The helper records each ruling with your card's wording in canon and the delegation policy. It also corrects every file that still says otherwise, such as CLAUDE.md's "bridge: owner only". Then it files the builds that make each ruling work:
- the archive path in the art tool;
- the idle watcher for the bridge;
- the leftover checker.

It also files one more item: stop agents from asking you questions they've already been delegated. That covers which work comes next and applying your rulings to matching copies, about 235 messages in 3 weeks. Those were already delegated and the habit never stopped.

The helper estimated these five at roughly 45–50 minutes of your time a week, once the builds land.

- **The bridge stays effectively yours,** because nothing can measure "20 minutes idle" yet.

All three builds are running now:
1. **Art archive path:** your newest sheet mark wins. An ✕ archives the picture, and one command restores it. Tests check both that a newer mark wins and that an older mark never beats a newer keep.
2. **Bridge idle watcher:** reads how long since your last keyboard or mouse input on Windows. If it can't read that, it treats you as present and refuses to take the bridge. It also refuses for flyer flight. It's tested against 19 minutes (refuse), 21 (allow), unreadable (refuse) and flyers (refuse).
3. **Leftover checker:** proves per kind that nothing reads a leftover and everything is pushed, archives it, and only then deletes it. Canonical world saves are always excluded. This first pass is a dry run only, listing what it would remove and how much space that frees.

The fourth helper is still reviewing today's game code. Live round 10 follows it.

It runs in stages, so the design rests on measurements rather than opinion:
1. **Evidence (running now).** A helper measures the answer to every question you listed, about 19 in all. Examples:
   - stale helpers still hanging around;

   - how efficient art sheets are per minute of your time;
   - when you're actually around: daytime phone check-ins you can't schedule, versus limited time at night;
   - which work never gets started, and whether anything has ever been declared done.

   For each question it also records whether anything answers it automatically today.
2. **Design, by Opus.** Five competing designs for the layer above Bench and Foundry, each with pros and cons tied to the evidence. It also covers:
   - the roles involved, including the specialists in the game-designer kit Anthropic released;
   - how to declare things done or frozen while ideas keep growing;

## Claude

For this one, letting me go was the right call. Your request already said what to produce, how to stress-test it, and what you'd do with it. A rewrite wouldn't have changed much.

**A simple rule for when to stop and improve the request first:**
- **Let me go** when the result is something you'll react to, like a write-up, a design or a set of options. A rough request costs little there, because your reaction does the steering.
- **Improve it first** when the result commits something hard to undo: code across many files, deletions, a lot of tokens, or a change to how the system runs. Also when you can't say how you'd judge the result. If you can't tell what a good answer looks like, neither can I.

**The one thing your request was missing:** a test for which design wins. Without one, the final judge picks whatever sounds best. I'll add one to the design stage now, using numbers we've already measured:
1. Your "keep going / status / steer" messages, about 31 a day today.
2. Items built but never accepted, 496 today.
3. Hours spent on tests that never catch anything. The evidence stage is measuring this now.
4. Bug reports on the focus mods: falling or not.
5. Whether "so how is the project going?" can be answered in one glance, without you assembling the picture yourself.

Every design will have to say which of these it moves, by how much, and how we'd know within two weeks. Tell me if you'd add or swap any of them.

You don't need to decide when to ask me to improve a request. If one is going to cost real resources and its success test isn't clear, I'll propose the sharpened version back to you before starting.

## Claude

The independent review of the two delegation builds paid off again. It found six real defects, all fixed and pushed.

- A machine nobody had typed on since boot read as "idle for days". Now it reads unknown and refuses.

The other is a picture kept only by an older keep on the same row. A fix has started. It also handles the case where archiving would leave something in the game with no texture: the ✕ then waits until a replacement exists and shows up in your digest, so there's never a gap in the game and never a permanent delete.
- **One known weakness:** someone writing a fake decisions file into the repo folder could forge a mark.

## Claude

I looked. My first keyword search badly undercounted, and so did a broader one. The honest answer comes in three parts.

**1. Explicit phrases are rare in the records.** Of 1,966 messages you typed from Oct 2 to Oct 27, about 49 (2–3%) contain an explicit frustration phrase, steady across the month. Examples:
- *"This is a big deal. Why did you ignore my directives?"* (10-08)
- *"Anything I've already ruled on shouldn't still say 'needs owner'…"* (10-12)
- *"I told you to handoff once you were done…"* (10-11)
- *"Clicking anything on the Murrelith kept closing their art as 'already ruled'… A real pain."* (10-16)
- *"Are you actually finding bugs tonight in the settings? Or has this been primarily ceremony?"* (10-11)
- *"I'm becoming concerned it's taking so long to Unbrake SteveDesktop."* (10-26)

**2. Keywords miss most of it, because you complain politely.** Lines like these match none of the obvious phrases:
- *"Some of my recommendations from before weren't yet done. That's surprising."*
- *"I'm shocked at all the art that wasn't regenerated."*
- *"Write python to explore these… rather than slowly waste time during the game."*

So 2–3% is a floor, not the real rate.

**3. The strongest evidence is our own rulebook.** Many of the rules agents read at every startup are your exasperation, written down after the fact:
- *"For the third time, do not do live testing of flyers without a human present."*
- *"I keep saying this. MAKE IT SO EVERYWHERE."*
- *"You don't need to keep rediscovering this."*
- *"Never ask that."*
- *"That file may be VERY old… do not accept stale info."*

Each was frustration that turned into a rule, and many are older than the records I can search: conversation records start Oct 2, and older transcripts are gone. So the system's main way of learning from frustration has been to add another line to the instructions. That's the integrator gap you described: nothing notices the pattern while it's happening.

**Next:** keywords can't measure politeness, so the real number needs a reading pass. A helper reads all 1,966 messages and tags each one as:
- repeating a ruling;
- something we should have known;
- an obvious fix you had to point out;
- "why so slow";
- ceremony;
- something that broke.

Each tagged message gets the cause that made it necessary. All four helper slots are busy (live round 10, the leftover checker, the evidence census, the art-archive fix), so this starts when one frees. Its numbers go into the evidence for the oversight design. The design's first job is to catch these causes before you have to type them.

## Claude

Recorded and pushed (`52ba5b9c`): **an ✕ now deletes a picture forever and bans it.**

- **What the ✕ does:** permanently deletes the picture's file. It also records a permanent ban covering that exact picture and anything that looks nearly the same.
- **Where the ban is enforced:**
  - review sheets won't show a banned picture;
  - installs refuse it;
  - redraws that come out too similar get thrown away and drawn again.
- **What it replaces:** the old "✕ archives" text is gone from the policy and canon. "Newest mark wins" still stands.
- **Leftover cleanup is separate.** Pictures you didn't ✕ still follow the cleanup rule: archive first, then delete. Only your ✕ means forever.

The build is filed (`ART_X_BUILD_DELETE_FOREVER_BAN`) and starts when a helper slot frees up. It will also measure how many pictures you ✕'d came back in the past, and by which route. That shows how much focus this has been costing you, and gives the ban a before/after number. I told the reviewer checking the art code that the ruling changed, so it won't push the code back toward archiving.

## Claude

You were right: frustration is common, just polite. Keyword searching found about 2% of your messages. Reading all 2,114 messages from Oct 2 to 27, a helper found:
- **27.5%** carry a sign that the system failed you: something broken, a repeat, or something we should have known.
- **16.5%** are specifically "I told you / you should have known / here's the obvious fix".

| Signal | Share of your messages | Recent trend (last 6 days) |
|---|---|---|
| Something broken or stale you had to report | 13.5% | rising: 10.8% → 18.0% |
| Repeating something already ruled | 9.4% | rising: 8.3% → 11.1% |
| Something we should have known | 6.1% | rising: 5.0% → 9.2% |
| Keeping it moving ("continue", "anything waiting?") | 5.4% | a floor; undercounted |
| An obvious fix you had to suggest | 3.4% | rising |
| Ceremony | 2.1% | rising: 0.7% → 4.0% |
| Too slow | 1.8% | falling since 10-13 |

All of these except "too slow" are rising.

**The five biggest causes:**
1. **Review screens that are stale or broken:** the dashboard, sheets showing old state, clicks that do nothing.
2. **Art defects the pipeline let through:** ladders, pipes, scale, perspective, wrong canon.
3. **Rulings not carried out or not remembered,** so you see the same thing again. The ✕ ban being built now goes straight at this one.
4. **Agents stalling or going idle,** so you have to poll.
5. **Token and helper overspend.**

**What this says about the fix:** your frustration does get turned into rules, about once a week. But repeats rose anyway, so the gap isn't missing rules. It's carrying out rulings and keeping review state current. That's direct evidence for the oversight layer you described: something has to notice "he's had to repeat himself three times this week about X" before you do.

How far to trust these numbers: one reader tagged everything. It agreed with itself on 35 of 40 rechecked messages, and a self-check flatters it, so treat the rates as approximate.

## Claude

You were right to be alarmed: part of it was broken, and I've fixed that part.

**What a clone is:** a complete private copy of the project folder that one helper works in. There's always the shared folder Bench and I work in. When several helpers run at once, each one makes its own copy, so it can't trip over files the others are halfway through editing. Each copy has two parts:
- **The project's history:** every past version. This is already shared between copies, so each copy's history costs only 20–40 MB.
- **The current files:** about 3.6 GB per copy, mostly art. Every helper checks out all of them, even if it only needs one folder.

So 65 copies × 3.6 GB ≈ 240 GB.

**Why there are so many:** helpers have been making 25–100 copies a day this week. One cause is my own briefs, which tell a helper to "work from a scratch clone if the shared folder has someone else's unfinished files", and that's nearly always true. Making copies is normal. Never removing them was the bug.

**What was broken:** on 10-18 you approved a cleaner: delete a copy after 24 hours if everything in it is pushed. It was built and runs every hour, but it was installed in report-only mode and never switched on. For 8 days it reported "I would delete 173 of these" every hour and deleted none.

**What I did:**
1. **Switched the cleaner on** (`630669a1`). From its next hourly run it actually deletes copies that are older than 24 hours and fully pushed, with no running process inside them. Its five safety checks still apply, so a copy holding the only copy of unpushed work is refused and reported.
2. **Filed the real size fix** (`SCRATCH_CLONES_SHRINK_SPARSE_NOT_FULL`): a helper copy should hold only the folders its task needs, not all 3.6 GB. Most tasks touch one mod.
3. **Filed a lesson:** a safety mechanism that only reports isn't switched on. Check what it actually runs, not what its design doc says.

The disk isn't in danger: 450 GB is free. This was waste and clutter, not an emergency. It also goes straight into the oversight design: nothing was watching whether the cleaner actually cleaned.

## Claude

Your ✕ now deletes a picture for good and bans it. It's built and pushed (`a9a6c82cd6`), and an independent review has just started on it.

**How often pictures you ✕'d came back:**

| How it came back | Count |
|---|---|
| The same picture shown to you again on another sheet | **232** |
| You had to ✕ it again more than an hour later | **366** |
| The exact same picture reinstalled | 21 |
| A redraw close enough to count as the same picture | 16 |

So mostly it was the sheets showing you things you'd already rejected.

**What the ban does now:**
- **Sheets:** a banned picture never appears.
- **Installing:** a banned picture is refused.
- **Redraws:** one that comes out as a near-copy of a banned picture is thrown out and drawn again, up to twice.
- **Undoing a ban:** only your typed words lift one.
- **Your digest:** lists every deletion and every ban.

**How it decides "near-copy":** two pictures count as the same when their fingerprints nearly match, including mirror images. Pictures you've kept are exempt from that near-copy matching. Across 400,000 pairs of pictures of different subjects, it wrongly matched 0.002%.

Four older tests that expected "✕ archives" were changed to your new ruling, "✕ deletes and bans". That brings them in line with the ruling and lowers no bar.

The reviewer is now checking the risky parts:
- whether a deletion can ever happen without its ban being recorded;
- whether any install path skips the ban check;
- whether a kept picture could let a banned one back in.

## Claude

The oversight design is done. All stages ran: research, five designs, two scathing GPT teardowns, an Opus repair, and the final arbiter.

`D:\Luke\dev\RimMandrake\design\RimMandrake\csuite_design_2026-10-26.md` (summary at the top)

**The main finding:** you're the integrator not because nothing watches, but because nothing *acts*. We already have instruments for 13 of your 19 questions. What's missing is a loop that reads them, acts, and checks the result. Today's examples:
- the game's speed dropped by two-thirds, and nobody saw;
- the cleaner sat in report-only mode for 8 days;
- rulings went uncarried;
- you sent about 31 "go / wake / continue" messages a day.

Both GPT teardowns killed the purely watching designs for that reason: they show the problem and you're still the one who has to make things happen.

**The three final proposals:**

| | 1. Operations Steward | 2. Delivery Producer | 3. Corrective-Action Supervisor |
|---|---|---|---|
| Controls | *that* things get done: stalls, waste, repeats | *what* gets done: the next player experiences, scope, freezing a mod | *what keeps going wrong*: one recurring failure at a time |
| Rhythm | twice daily and on every failure | weekly plan, daily dispatch | daily audit, at most 4 investigations |
| Best at | your "go" messages: 31 a day → ≤26; wasted test hours | "how is the project going?" and what "finished" means | your failure-signal share: 27.5% → ≤25% |
| Weak at | direction | day-to-day operations | delivery |

All three rest on the same base:
- **A dispatcher** that can wake a stalled window, hand it work, see the work done or retry, and record the result. It has to pass a 48-hour unattended test.
- **"Accepted" meaning something:** a pass on named scenarios, on a specific build, checked by someone other than the builder.
- **One assistant you can message from your phone at any hour.**

**The recommendation:**
1. Build the dispatcher first.
2. Then the Operations Steward, as that one assistant.
3. Add the Producer's planning discipline once carrying out work is reliable.

The reasoning: every failure we measured was a failure to carry out, not a failure to know. Foundry's future is decided later, by switching it on and off in trials once the dispatcher works, and the final call is yours.

**What still stands against it:**
- the dispatcher doesn't exist yet;
- "accepted" stays paperwork until test results are tied to a specific build;
- the first two weeks will mostly build measurements rather than show improvement.

GPT also caught arithmetic errors in our own evidence, which are now corrected and filed as a lesson.

**Five names for the assistant you'd talk to:**
- **Reeve:** the medieval officer who ran the estate's daily work for the lord.
- **Seneschal:** the steward who ran the household while the lord was away.
- **Tiller:** steady small corrections that keep the course. Fitting in a farming game.
- **Bosun:** calls the crew to work, so you never have to shout "all hands" again.
- **Vigil:** the watch that never sleeps.

After you've read that, are you ready for a question card on which proposal to build first and which name to use?

Still running: the independent review of the ✕-ban, and the hunt for what made the full game 3× slower. Live round 10 showed hoses aren't the cause.

## Claude

The independent review of the ✕-ban found five real defects. All are fixed and pushed (`58a9390e76`), and a new test fails 6 checks on the old code.
- **A ✕ could delete a picture without banning it.** The picture was deleted first, so if writing the ban failed, nothing stopped it coming back. The ban is now written first, and a half-finished ✕ gets completed on the next run.
- **An older lifted ban could cancel a newer ✕.** Each unban now lifts only the ban it names.
- **Restoring could bring back a near-copy of a banned picture.** It now refuses.
- **The art judge threw away good new drawings** whenever a banned picture appeared only in the comparison panels beside them, not in the drawing itself. Fixed.
- **A restored picture still read as archived.** Fixed and closed.

What it confirmed works: only your words lift a ban, and a picture you kept is never exempt from its own exact ban.

**Two gaps are filed, not fixed yet:**
- A ban taken from a picture that exists only in the game folder, not in the art store, misses its mirror image. So a mirrored redraw could slip through.
- The pre-push art check doesn't look at bans yet.

Still running: the hunt for what made the full game 3× slower.

My question from before still stands: after reading the oversight design, are you ready for a card on which proposal to build first and which name to use?
