## Claude

I need to correct what I said earlier: the game did not get 3× slower. The two readings came from different setups.
- **The 10-20 reading (354 TPS)** was a quiet save with one map and 3 colonists.
- **The 10-26 readings (112–194 TPS)** had a second, large test map loaded on top.

**Measured the same way, nothing changed.** That quiet save on today's full mod list runs at 355–358 TPS and 60 FPS, the same as on 10-20.

**What the second map really costs:** loading it makes each game tick about 3× slower. The biggest part is our own FlowWorks excavation system on the large map. It costs 0.87 ms every tick, with occasional single ticks of 158–172 ms.

The two smaller stalls filed earlier didn't reproduce. A frozen frame while paused, and slow ticks during the game's pre-tick step, both held steady in normal play. They stay open as partly verified until the two-map setup is rebuilt and they can be tested properly.

**Running now, offline:**
1. Making the excavation system only redo work where something changed, instead of re-scanning every tick. A test will prove its results are identical to the old full scan.
2. Stamping every speed reading with which save, maps, colonists and mod list it came from. Any comparison between readings from different setups will print "NOT COMPARABLE" instead of a number.

This is the same failure the oversight design describes: an instrument recorded a frightening number, nobody looked for weeks, and when someone finally did, the number was wrong. A lesson on comparing speed readings is filed.

The question about the oversight design is still open: are you ready for a card on which proposal to build first and which name to use?

## Claude

The excavation fix is built and tested offline, but not yet measured in the game (`f7fbbce8ca`).

**The cause:** every 60 ticks the wall-reach system treated any change in pathing as a change in connectivity. It then rescanned the whole map and re-ran every flood fill, at about 2 ms per fill on a 325×325 map. The pocket grid also re-flooded itself even when nothing it depends on had changed.

**The fix:** it now checks only the cells that changed, and rebuilds only when something real changed. A wall breach still forces a full rebuild.

**Offline results:** wall floods fell from 2,400 to 307, and pocket floods from 4,800 to 896. At every step the results were identical to a full rescan. All FlowWorks and player-truth checks pass.

**Speed readings now carry their setup** (`33d7b4b518`). Each one records the save, maps, colonists and mod list it came from. Comparing readings from different setups now prints "NOT COMPARABLE" instead of a misleading number, and it already refuses the Oct 26 vs Oct 27 comparison that misled us.

One wrinkle: an exact colonist count is part of that setup, so one extra colonist splits the readings into separate groups. The reviewer will decide whether to allow a small range instead.

**Running now:** an independent review of both. Mainly it's hunting for any map change that should trigger a rebuild but doesn't, because a missed one gives silently wrong results. After that, a live re-measure on the big map. The bar is the excavation system's own tick time: under 0.2 ms per tick, with no single tick over 20 ms. It was 0.87 ms per tick, with single ticks up to 172 ms.

The question about the oversight design is still waiting: which proposal to build first, and which name to use.

## Claude

The design has six roles to name. The first five are thinking roles, and in the first version several can share one agent. The sixth is a plain program, not an AI.

1. **The one you talk to: front desk and chief of staff.** A fresh Opus session each time you message it from your phone, so its memory never fills up.
   - **It answers** "how is it going?", "why is X slow?" and "what do you need from me?". Every fact it states is checked against the measurements before it's sent.
   - **It decides** what reaches you, when, and in what form. It batches questions into the times you're usually around and drops ceremony questions.
   - **It never** makes taste or north-star calls; those stay yours.
2. **The producer: the critical path and the plan.** It keeps the plan of what comes next in the game, sets work order and a limit on how many mods are in focus, and parks new ideas in an idea bank. It proposes which mods to focus on and when to freeze one; you rule on both.
3. **The operations steward: the machine itself.**
   - stuck processes and stale helpers;
   - turning on switches you've already approved, like the cleaner that sat in report-only mode;
   - disk, git and memory;
   - reboots scheduled in quiet hours;
   - pacing tokens across accounts, including filling Codex's unused allowance with art that's already been judged;
   - game speed.

   It never touches the game while you're playing.
4. **The test economist: testing as a cost.** It moves tests that never catch anything to the nightly run, refuses re-runs on unchanged code, and sets test-hour budgets. It never lowers a bar you set.
5. **The inspector: independent and adversarial.** It spot-checks closed work, re-files rework with a pointer to the original, and turns a lesson that keeps repeating into an enforced rule. It finds problems but never fixes or closes anything. That's the separation that caught the builders grading themselves today.
6. **The dispatcher: a program, not an AI.** It wakes a stalled window, hands it work, checks the work actually happened, retries, and records the outcome. It's the base all three proposals need, and it should pass a 48-hour unattended run before anything depends on it.

Around them:
- **You** stay the creative director: vision, taste, north-star bars, freezing and unfreezing mods, and choosing focus mods.
- **Bench and Foundry** stay the hands that do the work. Whether Foundry's window is still needed gets decided later, by a two-week on/off trial; the call is yours.

The five names from the write-up (Reeve, Seneschal, Tiller, Bosun, Vigil) were meant for role 1. The others fit some roles well, for example Vigil for the inspector and Bosun for the dispatcher.

## Claude

Here's the picture: https://claude.ai/artifact/8dMLHJKVSRd1ci4psfaP4a

It's laid out top to bottom, from you down to the builders:
- **You:** vision and taste, at any hour, from your phone.
- **The Conductor:** the one you talk to. It runs fresh on every message, so it never fills up, has no helpers, and answers from the measured numbers.
- **Leadership,** each with how often it runs and whether it has helpers:
  - **North Star Judge:** new. It rules whether a feature plays the way its north-star bar says, when that feature claims to be done. It uses judge helpers that never built the thing.
  - **Producer:** owns the plan and the critical path. It runs a weekly plan plus a daily list, and has no helpers.
  - **Operations Steward:** keeps the machines healthy. It runs twice a day and on alarms, with small fix helpers.
  - **Test Economist:** treats testing as a cost. It runs daily, using scripts rather than helpers.
  - **Inspector:** spot-checks closed work. It runs daily with re-check helpers, and only finds problems, never fixes them.
- **Dispatcher:** a program, not an AI. It runs every 2 minutes, and it's what replaces your "go / continue" messages.
- **Builders:** Foundry, around the clock, and Bench, when you sit down. **They're the only ones who build.**

I renamed one role for clarity: "Inspector" now means only "finds problems, never fixes them".

**Next step: three question cards, then building.** One of these changes a standing rule. CLAUDE.md says north-star bars stay yours. Giving them to a North Star Judge is the biggest change to how your authority works, so it needs a deliberate ruling with its limits spelled out, not just a name on a diagram.

The cards would be:
1. **The roles and names as drawn:** confirm them, or rename any.
2. **What the North Star Judge may decide alone, and what still comes to you.** For example, does a bar it can't judge clearly come back to you?
3. **Build order.** My recommendation is to build the Dispatcher first, with its 48-hour unattended test, then the Conductor, then the leadership roles one at a time, each judged on the numbers it's supposed to move.

Once you've looked at the page, are you ready for those cards?

## Claude

Welcome back. On the two merges you're weighing, I'd go opposite ways. I've updated the diagram to my recommendation, but your call decides both: https://claude.ai/artifact/8dMLHJKVSRd1ci4psfaP4a

**Judge and Inspector: keep them separate. Here I'm pushing back.**
- **The Judge says *yes*.** Before a feature counts as done, it rules that the feature meets its north-star bar. It's the gate.
- **The Inspector says *"are you sure?"***. After the fact, it checks what was accepted.
- **If one agent does both, its own passes go unchecked.** That's exactly what happened all day today. Every author that marked its own code clean got caught by an independent reviewer: the 162 "unused" pictures that were in use, the ✕ that could delete without banning, the lowered test bar. The Judge will be the most powerful role, because it takes over your north-star calls, so it most needs someone checking it. The diagram now says "audited by the Inspector" on the Judge.
- **I renamed it plain "Judge",** as you'd want.

**Operations and Economist: merge them. Here I agree with your instinct for simple.** One role called **Operations**, with two duties:
- **Keeps it running:** stuck processes, disk, reboots, game speed. It acts at once, on alarms.
- **Keeps it efficient:** one economy pass a day. It covers:
  - tokens on every account, including Codex;
  - test hours that catch nothing;
  - art redraw loops;
  - your attention spent on ceremony.

That broadens what the Economist would have covered, as you suggested. A split doesn't buy anything yet: both read the same numbers, both have the same authority (act on what's already ruled, set budgets, never lower a bar), and both act on the same machines.

The known risk of merging is that firefighting crowds out the efficiency work. That's easy to watch: if the daily economy pass is skipped or produces no action two days running, split the Economist back out.

**The design now:**
- the Conductor, which you talk to;
- four leadership roles: Producer, Judge, Inspector and Operations;
- the Dispatcher, a program;
- the two builders, Foundry and Bench.

When you've looked, I'll send the three cards: confirming the roles and names, what the Judge may decide alone, and the build order.

## Claude

You're right. The competing designs exist, but my diagram merged them all into one chart, which hid the choice. I've put them back at the top of the page, above the full role map: https://claude.ai/artifact/8dMLHJKVSRd1ci4psfaP4a

**How the competition went:** five designs went in, called Rounds, COO, Producer, Board and Inspector General. Both GPT teardowns killed the two that only watch, Rounds and Board, because they show you the problem and leave you as the one who makes things happen. The three that survived differ in which role leads:

| Shape | Leads | Fixes | Weak at |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1 · Run it** | Operations (incl. economy) | your "go" messages, wasted test hours | direction, design quality |
| **2 · Build the right things** | Producer + Judge | "how is it going?" at a glance, a real "done" | day-to-day running |
| **3 · Stop repeating mistakes** | Inspector + Innovator | the 27.5% of your messages that signal a failure | planning delivery |

All three keep the Conductor and the Dispatcher. The full map below the three shapes is all of them at once: the most capable, and the most to build. The arbiter recommended shape 1 first, then shape 2's planning discipline. My recommendation is to grow in that order (1, then 2, then 3), adding each role only when its number shows it's needed, so no role exists as ceremony.

**Your two points:**
- **Producer vs Judge: they look alike, but I'd keep them apart.** The Producer owns *what and when*: it wants progress. The Judge owns *good enough*: it doesn't care about the schedule. Merging them puts the agent that wants a date in charge of the bar, and that's the self-grading problem we caught all day today, one level up. In shape 2 they lead together, so you get both without a conflict of interest.
- **Innovator: added.** You're right that it was missing; nothing asked "what could be much better?" It has two passes:
  - **Process, twice a week:** proposes experiments on how we work, runs the reversible ones against a named number, and keeps or kills them on the results.
  - **Design, weekly:** reads the game design and asks what it lacks, what's out of place, misaligned or confusing, and what's an unneeded appendix.

  Design proposals go to the Producer's idea bank. Anything that changes a north-star bar or the vision comes to you. It leads shape 3.

When you've looked at the page, I'll send the cards: which shape to start with, the Judge's limits, and confirming the names.

## Lukas

Ok, how are Inspector and Judge different then?

## Claude

The Judge decides whether one thing is good enough to count as done. The Inspector checks afterwards whether the system's decisions, including the Judge's, were actually right.

| | **Judge** | **Inspector** |
|---|---|---|
| Question | "Does this feature play the way its north-star bar says?" | "Was what we accepted actually right, and why do things keep going wrong?" |
| When | **Before** done: a gate on each feature that claims to be finished | **After** the fact: samples work that's already closed |
| Scope | One feature against its bar | The whole system: builders, closes, rulings carried out, and the Judge's own passes |
| Looks at | The game: player experience, art, behaviour | The process: self-grading, repeated mistakes, rulings not carried out, stale state |
| Output | A pass or fail verdict that counts | Tickets for builders, and enforced rules from lessons that keep repeating |
| Power | Can say yes, which is what makes it "done" | Can never say yes or close anything; it only finds |
| Like | A referee calling the play | The league auditor reviewing the referees |

Today's examples fit them cleanly:
- **Judge work:** "Does the ladder lean against its wall?" and "Do the pipes join?"
- **Inspector work:**
  - "Five builders marked their own code clean and all five were broken."
  - "The cleaner sat in report-only mode for 8 days."
  - "The art judge passes things you later reject 74% of the time."

That last one is the Inspector auditing a judge.

**Why not merge them:** the Judge is the role that inherits your north-star authority, so it's the one most worth checking. If it also inspected, its own passes would go unchecked. That's the self-grading failure, at the most powerful seat.

**If you'd rather have one fewer role,** there is a simpler version that still holds up. The Judge's verdicts come from a fresh judge that never built the thing, which already happens. Then the Inspector's only standing job is a weekly sample of the Judge's passes against what you later accepted or rejected. That keeps the check while making the Inspector small. Shape 3 is the only one where the Inspector is large.

## Claude

- `Bash — Rename roles and add the scheduling table`

## Claude

I renamed them to **Auditor** and **Arbiter** throughout the page, and added a "Who decides when each one runs" table: https://claude.ai/artifact/8dMLHJKVSRd1ci4psfaP4a

**The short answer: no AI decides when to wake itself.** The Dispatcher, a plain program, runs every role from one schedule file. Agents can *request* a run by filing a ticket, and the Dispatcher queues it.

There are three kinds of trigger:
- **Your message:** the Conductor, every time.
- **The clock:** Operations twice a day, the Producer daily, the Auditor daily, the Innovator a few times a week.
- **An event:**
  - a feature claiming to be done wakes the Arbiter;
  - an alarm (a stuck process, disk, game speed, tokens burning too fast) wakes Operations;
  - a plan step failing wakes the Producer.

**When tokens run low,** roles slow down in a fixed order. The Innovator pauses first. The Auditor drops to a weekly sample. The Producer drops to weekly. The Arbiter batches its checks daily, and Operations runs on alarms only. The Conductor is never slowed, and the Dispatcher costs nothing.

**Who changes the schedule:** you set the starting file. After that, Operations' daily economy pass and the Innovator may propose a cadence change as an experiment, judged on the number it's meant to move. A reversible change is applied and the Conductor tells you about it. Anything that would silence a role comes to you.

The roles are now:
- the Conductor;
- leadership: Producer, Arbiter, Auditor, Innovator and Operations;
- the Dispatcher;
- the builders, Foundry and Bench.

When you're ready, the cards are which shape to start with, what the Arbiter may decide alone, and confirming the names.

## Claude

I agree. Bench and the Conductor are the same thing seen from your side: whoever you're talking to. I've merged them on the page, and Foundry is now the one autonomous builder: https://claude.ai/artifact/8dMLHJKVSRd1ci4psfaP4a

Two conditions make the merge work, and I've written both into the Conductor's card:

1. **It needs two modes, because the two jobs pull opposite ways.**
   - **Quick mode,** for a phone message: a fresh session every time, which answers from the numbers and passes your instructions to the Dispatcher. It never fills up.
   - **Session mode,** when you sit down: one long conversation like this one, with design, builder and live-testing helpers. It gets the live game first, and hands off at the end.

   A quick phone answer shouldn't carry the weight of a 6-hour workshop, and a workshop shouldn't restart on every message. This one agent switches between the two.
2. **It gets no exemption.** What the Conductor builds with you goes through the Arbiter and the Auditor exactly like Foundry's work. "I built it while talking to the owner" was the most common way things slipped through today.

That leaves eight roles:
- **You:** vision and taste.
- **The Conductor:** the one you talk to; it builds with you when you sit down.
- **Leadership:** Producer, Arbiter, Auditor, Innovator and Operations.
- **The Dispatcher:** a program that runs everyone on schedule.
- **Foundry:** the builder around the clock.

When you're ready, the cards are:
1. which shape to start with;
2. what the Arbiter may decide without you;
3. confirming the roles and names as drawn.

## Claude

The diagnosis is well grounded. The cure is mostly hypothesis. And one number says we're at real risk of the bubble you're worried about.

**What's solid: measured, and backed by long-standing practice outside this project.**
- **You are the dispatcher.**
  - About 40% of your messages just keep things moving: roughly 31 a day.
  - 27.5% of your messages signal that something failed, and that share is rising.

  Both come from reading 2,114 of your messages.
- **Things are built but never carried out.**
  - The cleaner sat in report-only mode for 8 days.
  - 98.8% of closed items record no check.
  - 123 "in progress" items have nobody on them.
  - A frightening frame-rate number went unread, and when it was finally read, it was a bad comparison.
- **Builders grading themselves fails.** Today every independent review found real defects in work its author had called finished: pictures that were in use, a ✕ that could delete without banning, a lowered test bar. FlowWorks went from 0 defects found in 2,744 self-reviews to 13 found this week once reviewers were independent. Keeping the maker separate from the checker is old, well-established wisdom, the maker-checker rule from accounting and quality assurance. That supports the Arbiter and the Auditor.
- **Delegation by class works.** You agreed with the recommended option most of the time, and five decision types are now delegated without incident.
- **The external advice we actually used** is mainstream:
  - Anthropic's guidance: keep state on disk, use subagents for isolation, compaction is acceptable.
  - Worker pools fed by a dispatcher.
  - Alerting when a budget burns too fast, rather than on every event.

**What's speculative: the parts that could be a fantasy.**
- **The eight-role structure is an org chart borrowed from human companies.** There's no evidence that more agent roles produce more progress. The only outside model we found, a community-built "game studio" kit with 49 agents, ranked its own heaviest process last.
- **Every benefit is a prediction.** None of the roles exists. The foundation, the Dispatcher, is unbuilt, and waking an idle window from outside has never been tested.
- **Every role costs tokens,** and we're already projected to run out before Wednesday's reset.
- **Our own evidence had arithmetic errors,** which the GPT teardown caught.

**The warning sign:** since yesterday, **70% of the 1,016 commits touched only process, ledger, design or tools, not mod content.** Your last redesign, the move to two windows on 8-27, was triggered by a measured **61%**. We're above the level that made you reorganize last time. Today produced real game fixes (hoses, Gimme Some Slack, the press, the sticks, pipes, excavation), but most of the effort went into the machine around the game.

**What I'd do about it:** build only the part the evidence supports, as an experiment that could fail.
1. **Build the Dispatcher.** It's small, it isn't an AI, and it removes your "go / continue" messages.
2. **Run it for a week and measure three things:**
   - your keep-going messages;
   - accepted items per day;
   - the process-commit share.
3. **Add a role only when a number shows the gap it fills.** The Arbiter and Auditor are the next best-supported, from today's evidence. The Producer and Innovator come later, if ever.

If after a week your messages haven't dropped and accepted items haven't risen, that's the evidence it was a bubble. We'd stop, and the baseline tag puts everything back.

The role design isn't wasted. The roles are worth keeping as names for jobs, but they shouldn't become agents until the evidence calls for each one.
